What It Was Like to Know Gloria Steinem
2026-09-14

What It Was Like to Know Gloria Steinem

TIME | 2026-09-14

Visit Original Website
Follow this section to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this tag to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Gloria Steinem in her New York City apartment on November 2, 1990. Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings I was a 33-year-old organizer when I first met Gloria Steinem in 2007. Despite being a women’s studies major in college, I had not fully grasped Gloria’s role in supporting grassroots women's organizations.  She had created the Ms. Foundation for Women to do just that, to fund and amplify women’s organizing, like the work I was doing with domestic workers in New York. Meeting her at their annual fundraising gala, I was moved by how kind, warm, and genuinely excited she was to learn about and support our efforts. “Do you know about Carolyn Reed?” she asked me. Reed founded the Household Technicians of America in 1971. Her organizing, along with the work of Dorothy Bolden and the National Domestic Workers Union in the South, catalyzed the inclusion of domestic workers in federal minimum wage laws after 40 years of exclusion from Roosevelt’s New Deal. I had never heard of Carolyn Reed or Dorothy Bolden. None of my women’s history classes had taught me about these two Black women who helped pave the way before me. But Gloria knew Ms. Reed well; she frequently enlisted Gloria in domestic workers’ efforts to gain respect and recognition as professionals. At the time, together with organizers from around the country, I was planning what would become the founding meeting of the National Domestic Workers Alliance. Gloria’s prompt helped us find our roots in the legacy of Black domestic workers organizing and leadership throughout the country, whose bravery has made our economy better for women generation after generation, a legacy our organization was founded to carry forward. This is who Gloria was. She recognized value and power where others didn’t.  She corrected the omissions that kept us from finding one another. A year after we met, I reached out to Gloria to let her know that we were at an important point in our efforts to pass the Domestic Workers Bill of Rights in New York State, the first comprehensive state-level legislation to extend labor rights to domestic workers, including new protections such as paid leave. We were planning a 24-hour vigil in front of the governor’s office. We communicated by email; she formatted her emails like letters, with all the accompanying formalities and indentations. We were five years into our campaign. Dozens of domestic workers had taken time away from work and sacrificed their wages to hold vigil. When Gloria arrived, she joined our circle on the sidewalk across from the governor’s office, listened to everyone’s stories, and when it was her turn to speak on our little red and white battery-operated bullhorn, she spoke of Carolyn Reed, how she was applauding our campaign from heaven, and would be walking alongside us on our march to victory. This was the beginning of a long friendship and mentorship. But Gloria’s specific form of mentorship included listening and sharing what she learned from other women, curated by her strategic mind and communicated with her unparalleled way with words. She did not give advice; she shared stories. She never saw herself as a mentor, as much as she felt mentored by others, always learning from the women she met, especially younger women. Gloria identified as an organizer more than an activist, because organizers re-organize relations of power to be more equal, and put power in the hands of everyday people. When I told her we were experimenting with neighborhood-based organizing circles to reach more domestic workers, she told me that previous movements of women gathered in circles of 13—small enough that everyone felt seen and heard, but large enough to feel the power of the collective. Like any good organizer, Gloria appreciated the chance to roll up her sleeves: she wrote letters to governors to help us get across the finish line in our legislative campaigns and called mayors to ensure that domestic workers would not be excluded from paid sick days legislation. If she was invited to speak at a press conference related to labor legislation, she would check with me to see how the bill impacted domestic workers. Several years into our friendship, I found her essay “Revaluing Economics” in Moving Beyond Words. Published in 1994—years before the National Domestic Workers Alliance was even the germ of an idea—she wrote about care as an essential resource that our economic model has never adequately valued, in no small part because of its association with women.  She argued that if we are to not only survive but thrive in the future, we must fundamentally protect and revalue care.  The last 15 years of my work have been deeply shaped by these ideas. More recently, I spent much of my time with Gloria through an intergenerational, multi-racial circle of women who affectionately call ourselves the “G Squad”—composed of a writer, two artists, a pro-democracy organizer, me, and Gloria. We gathered over the years to celebrate Gloria’s birthday, to strategize big moments in the women’s movement, or vet a new love interest in the mix. We learned about Gloria’s life and adventures from one another, and we learned about other women through Gloria. Much has been shared about the talking circles Gloria hosted in her home, but Gloria was part of dozens of ongoing circles of relationships with women like ours around the world. I wondered how she had the time for so many, including us.  She was one of the first people to send me a note when she learned I would be joining the Board of Trustees of the Ford Foundation. “I can't tell you how happy it makes me to know that your understanding and way of organizing will be there—you who don't hold a finger to the wind, but become the wind,” Gloria wrote. “It also makes me feel a little better about the way too early loss of Wilma.” She was referring to her close friend and former principal chief of the Cherokee Nation, Wilma Mankiller, who died of cancer with Gloria at her side.  Wilma was a legendary organizer and leader who also served as a trustee of the Ford Foundation. Gloria urged me to read Wilma’s speech, Context Is Everything. Gloria changed the context for women like me, our choices and our understanding of them. It was as if she lived on a different time and space continuum, one composed of circles of women throughout history, all “linked, not ranked.” Her true gift was helping so many of us see the hidden stories, ideas, lives, and contributions of women. She helped us locate our place in the story. In this world sorted by hierarchies of human value, at a moment when too many of our stories are being erased, may her memory be a reminder that we are not alone in the world or in history. We are part of a proud and vast legacy of women, seen and unseen, that continues to change the world. And that is exactly what Gloria would have wanted. How Escalating Attacks in Yemen Impact Global Oil Trade
2026-09-14

How Escalating Attacks in Yemen Impact Global Oil Trade

TIME | 2026-09-14

Visit Original Website
Follow this section to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this tag to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings A vessel anchored at Bab el-Mandeb Strait in the Red Sea, off the Yemeni coast southwest of the city of Taiz on July 24, 2026. Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings An escalating wave of attacks around the Red Sea has pushed oil prices above $100 a barrel, as Houthi territorial gains near the Bab el-Mandeb Strait and strikes on Saudi energy infrastructure threaten a route that has become a critical alternative to the Strait of Hormuz. On Sunday, Yemeni government forces, which are backed by Saudi Arabia, launched attacks against the Iran-backed Houthis along the Red Sea. The strikes were part of an effort to dislodge the Houthis after the rebel group captured the port city of Mokha and Perim Island at the mouth of the Red Sea, key positions that give the group a vantage point to threaten shipping through the Bab el-Mandeb Strait. The Houthis also launched missiles at several areas in Saudi Arabia, and in recent weeks attacked the kingdom’s oil infrastructure and shipping. The group declared a blockade on Saudi vessels in July, but said other countries’ commercial shipping can continue through the narrow Bab el-Mandeb. The escalation has pushed up energy prices around the world. It also potentially gives Iran leverage over the U.S. by threatening another maritime chokepoint, putting economic pressure on Washington just before the midterm elections. The attacks come as diplomatic efforts to end the nearly seven-month-long U.S. and Israeli war with Iran have dropped off. An anticipated diplomatic meeting between Gulf countries and Iran was postponed, Omani Foreign Minister Badr Albusaidi announced on Sunday. The meeting, meant to take place in Oman on Monday, would have been the first talk between Iran and Gulf Cooperation Council countries to discuss the Strait of Hormuz since a mid-June memorandum of understanding between the U.S. and Iran collapsed. The attacks have also reignited major fighting in Yemen’s yearslong civil war, which killed 150,000 people and created one of the world’s worst humanitarian crises. The U.N.’s migration agency said more than 82,000 people have been displaced by fighting in Yemen since the start of September. That includes more than 2,000 Yemenis who fled to Djibouti, which hosts a U.S. base on the other side of Bab el-Mandeb. Here’s how escalating attacks in the Red Sea have impacted energy prices and what could happen next. Saudi authorities said a projectile struck al-Tuwal in Jazan province, wounding two people and damaging a mosque, several buildings and vehicles. Earlier in the week, Saudi Arabia said a large wave of Houthi missile and drone attacks over four southern cities had injured 73 people. The Houthis said they launched ballistic missiles and drones at the Sharurah military base in Saudi Arabia’s southern Najran region, near the border with Yemen. Saudi Arabia has not confirmed damage at the base. The group alleged on Sunday evening that Saudi Arabia had conducted 58 airstrikes across Yemen during the preceding 24 hours, and 129 strikes across Yemen over the 48 hours before Saturday evening. Saudi Arabia has not confirmed those strikes. Inside Yemen, government forces launched strikes near Mokha, Dhubab, and elsewhere in Taiz, claiming they destroyed Houthi vehicles and weapons. After initially retreating from parts of the western coast, government units said they were regrouping late Saturday and would return to battle. The government-allied National Resistance said more than 500 of its fighters have been killed on the west coast over the past month, while more than 1,000 Houthis were killed in Hodeida and Taiz provinces over the same period. Oil prices climbed more than 2% on Monday. Brent crude futures opened at $108.23 a barrel in late Sunday trading, up $3.62 or 3.46%, while WTI futures opened at $103.20, up $3.15 or 3.15% from Friday’s close. U.S. diesel prices surpassed $6 a gallon on Friday for the first time on record, while oil gained more than 8% last week, with Brent surging over $100 a barrel for the first time since July. Saudi Arabia closed its East-West oil pipeline as a precautionary measure after multiple drones from Iraq damaged infrastructure in the Riyadh and Medina regions. No group has publicly claimed responsibility for the attack, although U.S. President Donald Trump said Iran was “probably” responsible. Saudi Arabia had recently rerouted around 4 million barrels per day—around 4% of global supply—through the pipeline to circumvent Hormuz. The attacks around the Red Sea have also added a substantial risk premium to transit through Bab el-Mandeb. Increased Houthi activity can discourage shipping even though the group said it will only target Saudi-linked vessels, Allison Minor, the director of the Atlantic Council’s Project for Middle East Integration and former U.S. deputy special envoy for Yemen, warned in a July analysis of the blockade. Tanker owners may weigh delaying voyages or charging higher freight rates, which could quickly raise costs for consumers. While some shipments could be redirected north through the Suez Canal or Egypt’s SUMED pipeline, both of those routes are estimated to carry smaller capacity than Bab el-Mandeb and could mean longer, possibly costlier, journeys for some cargoes. Experts say the duration of the disruptions will determine whether the current price increase becomes a sustained supply shock. If operations resume quickly, the pipeline’s effect could remain limited, June Goh, a senior analyst at Sparta Commodities, told Bloomberg. An extended shutdown, however, could force Saudi Arabia, the world’s biggest crude exporter, to cut production, Goh said. Saudi Arabia reportedly has enough oil stored at Yanbu to sustain current exports for five to seven days. The impact on global energy supply is exacerbated by severe restrictions through the Strait of Hormuz, which already pushed up energy prices around the world. The International Energy Agency (IEA)  estimated that Gulf oil exports in August were nearly half their pre-war level. Global oil inventories have fallen by 507 million barrels since February, the agency said. Thye May Ting, an analyst at Hong Leong Investment Bank, wrote in a research note that Brent is likely to remain elevated because of the combined pressure at Hormuz and Bab el-Mandeb, forecasting prices of $95 to $100 a barrel toward the end of the year. Using a baseline of $110 a barrel, economist Mohamed Shadi projected Brent could reach a peak of $120 a barrel if 25% of oil flows through Bab el-Mandeb were halted, $145 if 50% of flows were impacted, and $229 under a complete shutdown. Meanwhile, fighting near Hormuz has continued. A vessel transiting the Strait was struck by a projectile, causing a fire to break out onboard, the U.K. Maritime Trade Operations (UKMTO) reported on Sunday. Iranian state media said an Iranian commercial vessel was struck off its coast, killing one and wounding four. The IEA projects world oil supply to average 100.7 million barrels per day this year, a decline of 5.7 million barrels per day from last year, according to its September report. It had previously forecast a year-over-year decline of 4.3 million barrels per day in August. Saudi Arabia has reportedly pressed Washington for more military support, but the Trump Administration has so far resisted direct participation. Axios reported that Saudi Crown Prince Mohammed bin Salman called Trump twice on Thursday urging him to authorize U.S. strikes as the Houthis advanced along the Red Sea coast, but Trump refused. The U.S. had sent more than 100 military advisers to Saudi Arabia to provide military intelligence, according to CNN. Trump said Saturday that the Houthis had called his Administration. “They would much prefer not having us involved, and they’re letting most ships go through. There’s just one country that they’re not too happy with, and we’ll get that straightened out,” Trump said. The kingdom could independently intensify its air campaign and provide more support to Yemeni government forces on the ground, although Saudi air support reportedly failed to arrive during Thursday’s battle for Mokha despite requests from Yemen. A larger campaign could expose Saudi Arabia to more missile and drone retaliation from the Houthis, which it is likely keen to avoid after its costly intervention between 2015 and 2022 failed to defeat the group and prompted repeated attacks on Saudi cities, airports, and energy facilities. Saudi Arabia could also turn to Pakistan and Turkey for help under the Mecca Joint Defence Agreement, signed last month. Riyadh has not formally invoked the pact before, but it could request additional intelligence and technological support, Riaz Khokhar, a researcher at Islamabad’s Institute of Regional Studies, wrote in Al Jazeera. Islamabad, however, may be wary of domestic opposition and a confrontation with Iran, Khokhar wrote. Instead, it might push for a multinational naval coalition to protect Bab el-Mandeb. Saudi Arabia could also pursue diplomatic efforts to negotiate a cease-fire. Minor suggested in July that the Houthi campaign may be aimed at extracting concessions from Riyadh rather than creating an all-out war. Other analysts told Reuters that the Houthi gains could push Gulf governments toward accommodation with Tehran, especially since Iran’s talks with the U.S. have tapered off while fighting has intensified. Trump said Sunday that he still expects the Iran War to end this year, and that gas prices would “drop like a rock” once it did. Last week, Trump accused the Iranian government of prolonging the war in order to sabotage Republicans’ midterm prospects, but said Iran “can’t hold out any longer” and that the war would end “immediately after” the elections. He also floated the possibility of maintaining a U.S. presence to control Iranian oil. “We’ll ultimately get out [of Iran], ​unless ⁠we decide to stay and keep the oil like Venezuela,” Trump said. U.S. revenue from Venezuela, he added, “paid ⁠for ​the war many times.”

The CEO Moment: Airbnb’s Brian Chesky
2026-09-14

The CEO Moment: Airbnb’s Brian Chesky

TIME | 2026-09-14

Visit Original Website
Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Next year, Airbnb will mark its 20th anniversary. Through its evolution—from a few air mattresses placed on an apartment floor to one of America’s best-known American technology companies—it has been led by Brian Chesky. And at a time when Silicon Valley doesn’t champion introspection, Chesky, 45, is an outlier: someone who has become a prominent voice on company leadership and a frequent investigator of his own character. “The primary weakness of me as a founder running a company was people had to pay for my immaturity in my thirties,” he told me during a recent interview at Airbnb’s headquarters in San Francisco for our new interview series, The CEO Moment. “But I said it was a good long-term bet,” We spoke as the company, called out by critics as contributing to rising housing costs in the cities where it operates, was set to announce a number of new housing initiatives. You are one of three people who started the company in 2007. There aren't that many founders leading Fortune 500 companies. How do you think the fact that you founded this company shapes how you lead it today?The people that build companies tend to be more in the details, more decisive, bigger authority, …They tend to take bigger, bolder risks partly because they have the moral authority to. For example, I could—I wouldn't—but I could change the name of the company because I came up with the name of the company. Somebody who is a professional manager, I don't think they would ever dare change the name of their company. They would feel like it's not theirs to change. You’ve said that you think CEOs think about hiring wrong. Why is that?Because I thought about hiring wrong…When I was starting Airbnb…Sam Altman…said hiring should be 50% of your job. I'm not even sure it was 50% of his job. Has your thinking about the value of remote work shifted as we’ve gotten further from the pandemic?I have a view that if your office policy's is easy to explain, it might be wrong because it might be one size fits all. You’re vocal about the fact that you think leaders don’t need to have regular one on one meetings with their direct reports.I think it's not only unnecessary, it's probably inferior. And if you go through the list of great CEOs, almost none of them that I'm aware of do recurring one-on-ones… Now you can get a hotel on Airbnb. Why?I was ideologically against hotels. In fact, I think our first tag line was “forget hotels.” So why would I ever offer them? And so that was my party line for probably 10 years. In 2018, 2019 my stubbornness got the best of me and many of our customers told us that we want hotels. And ultimately I had to make a decision. You know if you're a brand, just because your customer wants something doesn't mean you sell it to them. You can decide no, go somewhere else…But we noticed there were three types of people. There are people that only book Airbnbs. There are people that only book hotels, but most people are willing to book both. You're building your own AI frontier lab separate from Airbnb. Why not build that within Airbnb and what will be the relationship between the two?I thought about building inside of Airbnb….I like to think that our shareholders know what they get themselves into, and I never sold them on us being a frontier company and spending billions and billions of dollars of capital expenditure and then spending huge sums recruiting these engineers or these AI researchers….The benefit to Airbnb and to me is I'm gonna learn a lot about AI, but it's also a totally different kind of culture. So I wanted to keep them separate. You’re announcing a number of new initiatives around housing. People say Airbnb makes cities more expensive. What role do you want the company to play in making places more affordable?I think Airbnb has been a place people pointed to over the last 15 years, especially the last decade…for reasons why cities are expensive. And so we have wanted to be part of the solution, not part of the problem. We're creating a $250 million fund to house the last dollar for projects. There's about 750,000 homes in America development projects that are stalled because they don't have the last dollar of completion ... .We want to do something called the City Index. We want to open source data around cities, policies, the cost of living, permitting  to basically show the cities that are most successful at lowering housing costs by having the best policies….We want to do the housing prize where we're going to do a million dollar prize every year for five years for any company, private or or nonprofit that has ideas around technology that can make housing development easier.

The Atlantic’s October Cover: Anne Applebaum on the “Empire of Grift” and the Revolution in American Foreign Policy
2026-09-14

The Atlantic’s October Cover: Anne Applebaum on the “Empire of Grift” and the Revolution in American Foreign Policy

The Atlantic | 2026-09-14

Visit Original Website
In The Atlantic’s October cover story, “Empire of Grift,” staff writer Anne Applebaum examines how far-right ideologues have transformed American foreign policy into a tool of private gain and antidemocratic reaction. Instead of making the world safe for democracy, she writes, U.S. foreign policy is rewiring the international order. Reporting from Brazil, Applebaum reveals that the United States is not the ally Brazilians once knew. No longer is American power deployed solely by trained diplomats and reliable officials, working through state channels; instead, “informal envoys, private companies, online grifters, and hardened ideologues both inside and outside the government matter more. Unlike their predecessors, these people do not talk about spreading democracy, and don’t want to build long-term relationships. They don’t care about America’s reputation. They are deploying the tools of American diplomacy and government for their own purposes, to promote their own business interests or their own ideological goals, because, suddenly, they can.” Though the Trumpists currently in charge of the U.S. federal government are frequently described as “isolationists,” Applebaum argues that that characterization is wrong. Many of these officials “don’t think of themselves as ordinary Republicans who won an election and will temporarily hold power until the next election. Nor are they mere appendages of Trump. They believe they represent something much greater: the vanguard of a movement that will alter not just America, but the world.” Applebaum continues, “Like earlier revolutionaries, they do not believe that their opponents are legitimate rivals … Their enemies are existential, and they are international: super-spreaders of a ‘wokeness’ plague, or proponents of a fictitious ‘censorship-industrial complex,’ or advocates of ‘regulation’ that will hamper American business, or self-hating promoters of ‘mass migration’ who want to drown Western civilization in a sea of brown-faced aliens. To maintain their power and defeat these enemies, the predators, like their Bolshevik counterparts, cannot rely on traditional diplomacy, because they don’t trust its practitioners.” The democratic world that came into being after 1945—a world based not exclusively on law, but on a set of mostly self-imposed restraints—is changing, Applebaum writes. In the post-1945 democratic order, Applebaum observes, “American power was largely derived from alliances that it refrained from exploiting. Long-term relationships were held to be valuable, because you never knew when you might need your allies.” That U.S.-led democratic order was of enormous benefit to this country and to the world: “America helped build eight decades of peace, prosperity, and eventually democracy in Europe and much of Asia, creating markets for U.S. companies and unprecedented support for U.S. power. This world is fast disappearing. Instead, following the president’s lead, different people inside and outside the administration are seizing the revolutionary moment in order to win zero-sum victories. Countries that hang back from this free-for-all know they will suffer. ‘If we’re not at the table, we’re on the menu,’ Canadian Prime Minister Mark Carney said at Davos earlier this year. He was right.” Anne Applebaum’s “Empire of Grift” was published today at TheAtlantic.com. Please reach out with any questions or requests. The Atlantic
George Lucas’s Next Great Epic Is an Art Museum
2026-09-14

George Lucas’s Next Great Epic Is an Art Museum

The Atlantic | 2026-09-14

Visit Original Website
What’s your story? The story that you live by, that your cells are geared to, even or especially when you’re not thinking about it? Do we create our own meaning, or should we be trying to discover the meaning that created us? Is it the tractor beams of heavenly love that hold us here, like God’s guy ropes, or are we adrift? Check out more from this issue and find your next story to read. When George Lucas, a long time ago in a galaxy far, far away, began writing the screenplay for Star Wars, he had a copy of Joseph Campbell’s The Hero With a Thousand Faces clamped, so to speak, between his teeth. For Campbell, a professor with a popular touch, life was nothing more or less than a summons to greatness. Across cultures, and across the history of the human imagination, he had discerned it, “the one, shape-shifting yet marvelously constant story”: the hero’s journey. Departure, initiation, return. Living is a heroic business, Campbell insisted. Stacked with trials, sure, and frightful reversals, but with the grail floating always ahead of us, calling us onward. Use the Force, Luke. Let go! And now Lucas has built a temple to story. The Lucas Museum of Narrative Art, which opens this month in Exposition Park, just south of downtown Los Angeles, is a swooping, brain-beggaring, super-sexy, twin-bodied leviathan of a megastructure, white as the gown of Princess Leia, half spaceship, half cetacean, all curves, Shangri-la for skateboarders if they could ever get inside. Co-founded by Lucas and his wife, Mellody Hobson, it hoists itself into twinkling California emptiness on an 11-acre site (formerly two parking lots) next to the Natural History Museum and the L.A. Memorial Coliseum. Walking toward it, you think: Jesus. Eighth wonder? Squillion-dollar folly? Architectural embodiment of some impulse from spiritus mundi? Entering it, you stop thinking altogether. As I passed from the dreamy light of the covered forecourt, a single lens-like portal in its ceiling (for maximum diffraction of heavenly tractor beams), and into the space-gobbling atrium, I was conscious only of a delighted vertigo. Like, awe. Like, wow. It was an old-school encounter with the sublime, not unlike the one I’d had as a 9-year-old, in 1977, in a cinema in Cambridge, England, watching the first scene of Star Wars. You remember the first scene of Star Wars. A tiny fleeing Rebel ship zips past us into the star-pricked abyss, laser bolts pinging around it, and then, and then, Darth Vader’s Star Destroyer drones overhead, shears off the top of the screen, the viewer’s eye hauled powerless and silently wow-ing for what feels like miles along the faceted grayness of its Imperial underbelly. Agog in my cinema seat, I was a tiny, grubby Shelley, and before me was Mont Blanc. Lucas and Hobson thought about building their museum in San Francisco; there were prolonged negotiations to build it in Chicago; but it’s landed in the right place: fictive L.A., the Kingdom of Story, where even now, in coffee shops and one-room apartments across the city, on laptops sweating with hope, at least 400 screenplays are being written in accordance with the immemorial template mapped out by Campbell and industrialized by Lucas. Look around: Everyone here is on their hero’s journey. So in Exposition Park it sublimely squats, the Lucas Museum, flaring its contours into the L.A. void. Why is it so bloody enormous? Because it has to be. Because larger and grander and deeper than the campus itself—with its library, two movie theaters, 100,000 square feet of gallery space, and sculpted stretch of public parkland—is the wobbling master-idea of Lucas himself: a museum of narrative art. Of images that tell or contain or project or smell like a story. Which means … pretty much everything in the history of art that isn’t abstract. Which means the story of art itself, from the hovering aurochs and Paleolithic handprints of the cave paintings of Altamira (presented here in a glowing full-size reproduction) to the gods-and-monsters heavy-metal dream time of Paul Raymond Gregory. Which means comics, murals, film, Conan the Barbarian and Frida Kahlo, Andrea Solario’s Salome With the Head of John the Baptist and Robert Crumb’s Book of Genesis. (First frame: God holding a big old hairy ball of Chaos in his hands.) Which means Richard Ansdell’s The Death of Sir William Lambton at the Battle of Marston Moor next to a reproduction of Gustave Courbet’s A Burial at Ornans. Both paintings are frieze-like in structure: In the former, the body of an expired or expiring nobleman is conveyed through a turmoil of cattle, dogs, sheep, and milling cavalry; in the latter, a gang of grim-faced locals assembles round an open grave, down at the village cemetery. In both paintings, death is a kind of antimatter, churning the scene. Am I making it sound random? It’s not random. Some of the galleries are organized around the foundational myths of American culture—work, family, sports—while others are dedicated to a single artist (Maxfield Parrish, for example). It is, however, wildly democratic. Star Wars, as movie geeks know, is a brilliant hodgepodge of influences: Buck Rogers, Kurosawa, The Dam Busters, high culture, trash culture, the lot. At the Lucas Museum, this is part of the character of the collection—everything that tweaked, tickled, or ran its buzzing lightsaber through the imagination of young George Lucas is represented here. The galleries dedicated to comics and cartoons have a particular energy, pulp art exploding off the walls. Downstairs, meanwhile, on giant screens, are giddyingly depthful high-res photographic re-creations of the Sistine Chapel and, nearby, the Hagia Sophia. The effect is slightly Vegas, that throb of alien vividness, that feeling of signs coming loose from their moorings, the Sphinx gazing blandly across Las Vegas Boulevard—but I love Vegas, and America has taught me to love that feeling. Lucas is also a major collector of that singular populist and freakish master of compacted visual storytelling, Norman Rockwell. I was delighted to be able to stand in front of The Connoisseur—which features a well-dressed older gentleman, seen from the rear (Rockwell loved his rears), his body somewhat torqued with interior tension, standing in front of a large Jackson Pollock–style cosmic splatter. Doing so, I participated, as it were, in its sight gag: a man looking at a painting of a man looking at a painting. Very Rockwellian. The amplitude of all of this is not for everybody. Writing in the Los Angeles Times in 2017, after it had been announced that the museum was coming to L.A., the critic Christopher Knight protested that narrative art was a “made-up category.” He scorned the definition then on the museum’s website—“Narrative art is visual art that tells a story”—as a “perfect tautology.” Linguistically, he was quite correct, but then again, I’m not sure how you could fail to have a good time at the Lucas Museum, splashing through the gorgeous spaces, bouncing from Diego Rivera to Frank Frazetta. More than that, the conceptual bagginess and ambition and all-gatheringness of the collection is its own statement, as American as it gets. Consciousness is so squeezed right now (close your eyes for two seconds and you’ll feel it) and thieves work the levers of the world, but you leave this museum with a sensation that rumbles in the body for hours—a sensation of art as the most illustrious kind of counterprogramming, art as the vehicle for the real story, which is imagination and connection, which is life. “The effect of the successful adventure of the hero is the unlocking and release again of the flow of life into the body of the world,” Campbell wrote. Is this what he’s done, 82-year-old Lucas? Streamed the Force right into Exposition Park? Dinged by his Rockwells, donged by his replica Michelangelo, I’m going to say yes. It’s been 21 years since Revenge of the Sith, but this is tremendous storytelling—a killer final act. The hero kneels and opens his hands, and look what comes out: a great and crazy museum, flexing its fins, firing its lasers, swimming like an amphibian through time. * Lead image sources: Cindy Ord / Getty; FlixPix / Alamy; LMPC / Getty; Rob Kim / Everett Collection; SOPA Images / Getty; Universal History Archive / Getty This article appears in the October 2026 print edition with the headline “That’s No Moon.”

What Trump Doesn’t Understand About Xi
2026-09-14

What Trump Doesn’t Understand About Xi

The Atlantic | 2026-09-14

Visit Original Website
President Trump will host Xi Jinping for a state visit on September 24, once again putting his claimed bonhomie with the Chinese leader at the center of the relations between the two superpowers. But Trump’s insistence on the importance of this personal rapport shows just how little he understands his adversary. I have been studying Xi ever since he became China’s heir apparent, and I became an analyst at the CIA in the waning days of the George W. Bush administration. I spent years deciphering Xi for U.S. presidents as the intelligence community’s top expert on Xi. And I spent two years as the director for China at the National Security Council, where I staffed all of President Biden’s interactions with Xi and coordinated diplomacy with China across the U.S. government. For a decade now, Trump has oscillated between courting and skirmishing with Xi. After his first term, Trump called Xi a friend and described him as someone he knew well. His experience with Xi seems to have led him to believe that he has unique, firsthand insights into China’s leader that his staff and advisers lack; in the past year, he has excluded even his close confidants, such as Jared Kushner and Steve Witkoff, from intensive diplomacy with China, and has acted practically as his own China desk officer. Trump’s go-to stratagem for gaining leverage with Xi has been to be unpredictable, or at least to try to be. Yet in Trump’s second term, Xi has surprised Trump more often than Trump has surprised him. That is because Xi’s modus operandi, at home and abroad, is that of a jack-in-the-box: He winds up patiently over years, sometimes decades, and then pops at the propitious moment. The result is always startling. Xi rose to become China’s leader in large part by presenting himself as having the steady hands needed to restart stalled economic reforms without upsetting the comfortable gentlemen’s agreements that kept the Communist Party’s factional rivalries in check. But once Xi secured power, he launched a blistering campaign against the very party elites who had pulled him up. Xi’s steeliness has caught Trump off guard, just as it did his Chinese colleagues. At the time of Trump’s first term, Xi was accustomed to playing politics as a quiet, intense poker match, in which the Chinese leader read the opposing players and considered his own cards with characteristic subtlety. Trump, meanwhile, approached politics and policy making more like a gambler on a hot streak at one of the casinos he owned. During his first encounter with Trump, at Mar-a-Lago in 2017, Xi learned about U.S. air strikes on Syria over a beautiful piece of chocolate cake. He spent the years in between Trump’s first and second terms winding up to deal with a president whose M.O. he now understood—and preparing to pop up when the moment was right. That moment came early in Trump’s second term. In April 2025, the U.S. president rolled out his “Liberation Day” tariffs, which included some that amounted to a de facto embargo on China. Xi surprised Trump by being the only world leader to retaliate rather than conciliate. Trump was ultimately forced to fold. In the run-up to the two leaders’ summit in Seoul last fall, Xi again surprised Trump’s team—this time by unfurling an expansive set of export controls on rare earth elements, which ultimately prompted the U.S. to roll back its own export controls on China. Beijing’s willingness to escalate was a remarkable departure from a previous pattern of downplaying differences ahead of such summits, and it underscored that Xi was more interested in curtailing Washington’s ability to target China than in striking a deal. Accordingly, the meeting in Seoul, and Trump’s subsequent visit to Beijing in May, produced little beyond relatively minor commercial agreements affecting specific firms and sectors. The Trump administration, undoubtedly disappointed by the lack of any meaningful deal to emerge from these summits, chose to emphasize Trump’s efforts to deepen his rapport with Xi. The president evidently believes this personal relationship will allow him to secure deals that advance American interests. This expectation has no observable foundation in Xi’s 14-year rule. Even by the standards of authoritarian rulers, such as Russia’s Vladimir Putin, Xi has shown himself to be unsentimental about personal relationships—including those with members of his own inner circle, to say nothing of the leader of China’s principal geopolitical rival. Xi is very different from Putin in this regard. Whereas Putin takes extravagant risks on the international stage but is loath to hold his associates accountable for incompetence, Xi is just the opposite. He is calculating and measured in international politics but pitiless in jettisoning even longtime associates for perceived malfeasance. In January, Xi dismissed his military’s top officer, General Zhang Youxia. The relationship between Xi and Zhang went back decades, extending not just to their childhoods but all the way back to their fathers, who were comrades in arms in China’s ferocious civil war. Four years ago, Xi deferred Zhang’s retirement, keeping him on board to help manage the world’s largest and second-most-capable military. We do not know what motivated Xi to oust Zhang, but given the history between the two men, the act was as brutal and unexpected as anything in a season finale of Game of Thrones. Xi’s approach to Washington has been similarly dispassionate. During Trump’s visit to Beijing, the American president lavished praise on Xi, only for the Chinese leader to respond with stoic indifference. Read: What the floods say about Xi Jinping Trump’s desire to parlay personal charisma into deals may be one of his least idiosyncratic qualities. In fact, there is something about the impulse that is quintessentially American. Even—perhaps especially—for a Beltway outsider, ascending the American political ladder requires a tremendous amount of glad-handing and personal magnetism, if not charm. Those who reach its pinnacle often assume that the same qualities will serve them on the international stage. Joe Biden rose through the Senate, which required cordiality and the ability to strike deals with rivals. George W. Bush and Bill Clinton were known for their affability. Overbearing friendliness might even be described as our national affect. By contrast, Xi grew up in a system that punished openness and personal dynamism. His father’s boss and mentor, the reformer Hu Yaobang, whose death in 1989 sparked the protests in Tiananmen Square, was known for his outspokenness, which played a crucial role in his political demise. Hu’s ouster extinguished freewheeling politicking from the ranks of China’s leadership ever after. Going into his next encounter with Xi, Trump seems to genuinely believe that he has the upper hand. Yet an unsettling split screen has accompanied every summit of his second term. In Seoul, Xi walked into the meeting having just outlined the five-year plan for China’s development through 2030. By contrast, Trump was in the midst of the longest government shutdown in U.S. history. In May, Xi hosted Trump for a meeting that Trump had earlier postponed because of the war in Iran, which was going badly. Xi now plans to follow his state visit to Washington with a major party meeting to lay the groundwork for his fourth five-year term, which will start next year. Trump, meanwhile, will be at best a lame-duck president within weeks of the summit—or worse, if the Democrats are able to take back the House or the Senate. The contrast evident at these meetings with Trump has surely reinforced Xi’s view of the United States as a declining but dangerous power, and of China as an ascendant one. Xi believes that time is on his side, so he does not need a deal. He’s happy to perpetuate an uneasy stalemate through a pantomime of rapport with Trump. In the meantime, Xi is winding up for the fifth U.S presidential administration of his tenure, whatever it may bring. All statements of fact, opinion, or analysis expressed are those of the author and do not reflect the official positions or views of the U.S. government. Nothing in the contents should be construed as asserting or implying U.S.-government authentication of information or endorsement of the author's views.
The Atlantic
The Biggest Obstacle to Universal Child Care: Babies
2026-09-14

The Biggest Obstacle to Universal Child Care: Babies

The Atlantic | 2026-09-14

Visit Original Website
Last year seemed like a pretty big one for breakthroughs in child care. In New York City, Zohran Mamdani’s promise of publicly funded care for young kids helped him win the keys to Gracie Mansion. Officials in Connecticut created an endowment to make care more affordable in that state. And New Mexico became the first state to commit to free child care for all of its residents. But as policy makers have begun hammering out the details of these ambitions, they’ve hit against a stubborn and formidable obstacle: babies. Any child-care provider will tell you that “affordable infant care” is an oxymoron. Because babies need a ton of individual attention, they are the most demanding group to care for in formal settings. Most states require babies to be looked after by more workers and in smaller groups than those mandated for older kids; a day care’s infant room will typically have one caretaker for every three to four babies, whereas a pre-K teacher might look after more than twice that number of children. Many states and cities also have costly space prerequisites for infant care. In New York City, for instance, a day care’s infant room must have sprinklers installed and be on the ground floor to allow for easy evacuation. All of this drives up the cost of care; one estimate placed the monthly cost of “high-quality” infant care at more than $2,400, a price that only well-off families can afford. At many child-care centers, the tuition paid for older children subsidizes the babies. “Infants are cute little loss leaders,” Josh McCabe, the director of social policy at the moderate-leaning Niskanen Center think tank, told me. Some programs—because they subsist on razor-thin margins and can be prone to worker shortages—stay clear of babies altogether. (Consider 2-K, Mamdani’s new child-care program for the general public; as the name implies, it does not cover children under 2, even though families with very young children need affordable care as much as anyone else.) This can make infant care a rarity in the places that need it most, including low-income neighborhoods, where the cost of baby care far outpaces what many parents can pay, and rural areas, where day cares serving children of any age are few and far between. States and cities are thus likely to “hit a wall” when trying to ramp up infant care, Brian Dew, an economic researcher who recently wrote a report comparing the U.S. and Nordic child-care systems, told me. Yet one fair, cost-effective solution could provide relief both to parents and to governments seeking to provide high-quality care: Pay families to keep babies home. That idea might at first sound odd. But almost every country with universal child care provides a version of this benefit, which allows parents to spend more time with their young children or pay, say, grandparents or neighbors to do it. In the United States, this sort of allowance could help reduce the burden on governments trying to implement universal care and give parents with babies an option that many say they want. It’s also a proposal with growing appeal among a politically diverse set of policy makers. Read: The unglamorous truth about the average tradwife Traditionally, incentives to keep more young kids (and, by extension, their mothers) home have been associated with conservative politicians. Progressives have focused on increasing access to child care so that parents can work. More broadly, the notion of paying to keep some children home, specifically as part of an effort to offer universal child care, has been “an under-explored area of family policy,” Pete Nabozny, the director of policy at the advocacy organization the Children’s Agenda, told me. But in recent years, the idea has been catching on. In 2024, Democratic Representative Ro Khanna of California proposed a child-care plan that would not only reduce the cost of formal care but also compensate caregiving relatives and provide a stipend for stay-at-home parents opting out of formal care. In July, Project 2029, the progressive effort to create a policy agenda for a future Democratic administration, released a proposal to allow parents to choose between free child care or a cash benefit of $1,000 a month “to provide care at home or help pay someone they choose to provide care.” And in a forthcoming report, the Niskanen Center suggests reviving a largely defunct effort from the 1990s and early 2000s, when Montana and Minnesota tested programs offering low-income parents the chance to stay home with their babies while receiving a stipend of about the amount that would have gone toward formal care. Such plans should not be confused with the Trump administration’s recent proposal to redirect dollars from a finite pot of child-care funds to married stay-at-home parents—a move that is almost certain to give fewer, not more, families help in paying for child care. As Jessica Grose noted in The New York Times, it is especially likely to penalize single mothers. And it seems designed to coax married mothers out of the workforce. Meanwhile, state and city officials who earnestly want to offer more families child care continue to seek solutions and shortcuts to create more spaces for babies. Last year, Vermont increased the reimbursement rates paid to providers for infants and toddlers as part of a bid to expand care for infants; this does appear to have helped generate additional slots for very young children, though still not enough to meet families’ needs. This year, New Mexico reduced zoning barriers to make it easier for licensed child-care programs to operate in private homes, where most infants are cared for. Then there is Mamdani’s plan for New York City—which so far mostly sidesteps the issue of infant care. His administration has said that it does not plan to extend universal care to infants during the current term, which some policy experts warn is shortsighted. Research on the national expansion of universal pre-K programs found that when public child-care programs for older kids move into a neighborhood, providers serving younger children tend to shift to serving the older, more profitable kids, intensifying care shortages for the youngest. Nabozny said that researchers closely watching the rollout in New York are concerned that Mamdani’s plan may replicate this trend: that it will make infant care even scarcer and, where it is available, more expensive. This is where offering parents a paid option to care for their babies at home—while also providing more infant care at centers and home-based day cares—starts to make sense. Such an incentive would help take the pressure off formal child-care systems to create more infant-care slots than may be possible. Reducing the number of infants in care outside the home would also make it easier to keep ratios lower for babies in traditional programs, giving kids a better shot at receiving the close attention they need. It would put money in the pockets of families who have recently welcomed a baby—an economically precarious time of transition, when expenses spike and working outside the home can be unfeasible for some mothers. And it would lower costs for states, cities, and counties looking to offer universal care programs, increasing their odds of success. But arguably the most crucial benefit is that the paid option would give parents something they say they desire, Alyson Silkowski, a senior policy adviser at New America and a co-author of the Project 2029 proposal, told me. Silkowski co-wrote a recent New America report based on a nationally representative survey that oversampled low-income parents; it found that, given the choice, more than a third of parents with children younger than 12 months said they would like to care for their babies themselves throughout the day—a preference that declines once a child turns 1. More than half of those parents want their baby to be looked after by at least one of the child’s parents, Silkowski later wrote to me. Only 9 percent preferred formal care. “There is this higher level of discomfort,” Silkowski said, “in not being with your youngest child during those first 12 months.” Most countries with universal child care make it easier for parents to stay home with their babies by using some combination of generous paid-parental-leave programs and child- and home-care allowances. In the Nordic system, public child care typically does not start until age 1, when paid leave tapers off. Parents in Finland can opt for a home-care allowance to continue caring for their children until they are 3, and almost all families take this option for at least a few months; many say that 1 year of age is too early for a child to enter formal care. In the United States, by contrast, few parents have access to paid family leave to care for a newborn, and many can’t afford unpaid leave, prompting a lot of parents to either leave the workforce or return before their family feels ready. But some precedents do exist for paying low-income parents and their relatives to care for children in lieu of enrolling in subsidized care. New York is one of several states with a popular “friend, family, and neighbor” program that lets families who qualify for subsidies hire someone they know for care. And there was the pilot program in Montana and Minnesota during that earlier welfare-reform era. McCabe, of the Niskanen Center, told me that although both Democrats and Republicans have proposed similar programs in the years since, those plans never took off—partly because they didn’t fit neatly into the policy buckets of welfare, paid leave, or child care, which prompted some advocates to fear that such programs would divert funds and attention from their core causes. In addition, some critics have worried that paying parents to keep babies at home could prevent mothers who want to return to work from doing so. (Research in other wealthy countries suggests that policies such as paid parental leave actually boost women’s workforce participation, though women with children under age 3 still tend to work less than full-time.) To Nabozny, the idea that a child-care plan should come equipped with a stipend option for the youngest children is a “no-brainer.” “It’s kind of crazy to say the government will pay some other person $25,000, $30,000, whatever the cost of infant care is for a parent to go to work, but we won’t pay that parent a dollar to provide it themselves,” he said, especially “when that is often the family’s preference.” In recent years, a growing number of advocates and policy makers have argued that care work ought to be treated as an essential piece of American infrastructure. The secret to strengthening the country’s formal child-care infrastructure—counterintuitive as this may sound—may be to pay some parents not to use it.
