The Download: a “God-driven” cryptocurrency and a solar engineering roadmap
2026-09-10

The Download: a “God-driven” cryptocurrency and a solar engineering roadmap

MIT Technology Review | 2026-09-10

Visit Original Website
This is today's edition of The Download, our weekday newsletter that provides a daily dose of what's going on in the world of technology. When Eli Regalado first heard God speak to him, he wondered whether he was hallucinating. According to Eli and his wife, Kaitlyn, He told them to get married, buy a house, and start having kids. Then in 2021, divine guidance steered them in an unexpected new direction: crypto. That October, the Regalados later testified in court, they received holdings in a little-known digital coin. “Take this to my people for a wealth transfer,” Eli heard God say. Over time, they came to believe that He wanted them to launch their own coin. The Regalados created INDXcoin, which they promoted through family, friends, and contacts in evangelical Christian circles. In all, more than 500 people handed over more than $3 million. But within a year, the project collapsed. Investors lost it all, leaving many to wonder where the funds went and whether they had fallen victim to an elaborate fraud. Read the full story on the collapse of a pastor’s “God-driven” cryptocurrency. This article is part of the Big Story series, the home of MIT Technology Review’s most important and ambitious reporting. You can read the rest of the series here. The story was produced in partnership with Type Investigations and with support from the Fund for Investigative Journalism. Scientists have spent half a century exploring whether we could counteract climate change by releasing reflective particles into the stratosphere, mimicking the cooling effects of volcanic eruptions. But even after hundreds of studies, we still don’t know how well it would work or what else it might do—and there’s no systematic plan for clearing up that uncertainty. Reflective, a research organization, has now attempted to fill that gap. The San Francisco nonprofit has published a detailed road map of the experiments, studies, and infrastructure that it says would be needed to make informed decisions about the use of solar geoengineering, MIT Technology Review can reveal. Find out what it would take to make informed decisions about solar geoengineering. Throughout the history of the computer chip, engineers have treated waste heat as an inevitable cost of a calculation. Hannah Earley, however, thinks it’s a design choice. Earley, 31, is cofounder and CTO of Vaire Computing, which builds chips that recycle energy usually thrown away as heat, a strategy known as reversible computing. The approach could make data centers (and our laptops and phones) much more energy efficient. Last year, Vaire announced a key breakthrough: a chip with a resonator that recovered more energy than it lost, even after the energy needed to power the component was taken into account. Here’s how she plans to bring an old idea about energy-efficient computers into the future. Hannah Earley is one of the computing and robotics honorees on our 35 Innovators Under 35 list for 2026. Meet the rest of them here, or explore the full list across the biotechnology, AI, computing and robotics, and climate and energy categories. The US energy storage market is growing at a record pace, which could shore up the grid and cut emissions. Crucially, this is all happening with the help of cheap Chinese batteries, which the Trump administration is trying to phase out. Reducing reliance on any single source of crucial energy technology makes sense. But the tension raises a broader question for me: how much should countries take advantage of cheap, available tech, and how much should they cut themselves off from foreign sources to develop their own, even if it costs more? MIT Technology Review
Powering AI is an architecture problem
2026-09-10

Powering AI is an architecture problem

MIT Technology Review | 2026-09-10

Visit Original Website
On July 22, 2026, a transmission line fault in Ashburn, Virginia—the heart of the world's largest data center cluster—knocked more than 3 gigawatts of load off the grid in seconds. And it wasn't the first time. Two years earlier, a single failed surge arrester dropped roughly 60 Virginia facilities and 1,500 megawatts at once. No one could anticipate so much uniform load responding to grid faults the same way, at the same time. The AI power debate is mostly about generation: more turbines, more solar, more transmission. The grid needs more electrons. But the outages in Virginia weren't supply failures; they were architecture failures. And a giant wave of interconnections is arriving on that same architecture, putting grid reliability at risk. It's a problem nobody wants to own. The grid was built around predictable loads: steel mills, refineries, and houses at dinnertime. Different load sizes, same process—drawing power smoothly, misbehaving occasionally, and recovering gracefully. But AI data centers don't behave that way. An AI campus can swing 70% of its load in milliseconds during a training run, then trip offline just as fast at the first sign of trouble upstream to protect billions in compute. Each is rational alone. Together, at gigawatt scale, they're a problem the grid has never solved—and the next wave of data center campuses is planned at exactly that scale. The standard data center power stack hasn't changed in decades. Medium-voltage power arrives, transformers step it down, low-voltage uninterruptible power supply (UPS) units condition it, and it reaches the racks. Push that design to AI scale, and it cracks in three places. First, the UPS sits deep inside the building, close to the racks. But its batteries are an undersized spare tire, designed to handle an outage for a few minutes, not to absorb load swings this fast and volatile around the clock. Second, the UPS spends most of its life in bypass. Legacy converters waste enough power that operators run in eco-mode: A static switch feeds the racks directly from the grid and nothing filters in either direction. The compute's swings go out raw, and grid transients—sub-millisecond events that can damage or take down equipment—come in too fast for any switch to catch. Third, the protection logic was written when "large load" meant 50 megawatts. This protection logic can't see the grid it is now a part of, so when trouble hits upstream, it does exactly the wrong thing: it drops out. In the 2024 Virginia event, most of the lost load traced to protection schemes that count voltage dips and disconnect on the third one—as designed, at the worst moment. This isn't sloppy engineering. It's careful engineering the load has outgrown. Move it up—from 480 volts to medium voltage (13.8 kilovolts and higher), the voltage large sites draw from the grid. Move it out—from the data hall to modular enclosures near the substation so the building holds only compute and the cooling that keeps it alive. Move it into the path—instead of a battery that watches and reacts, a system every electron runs through, all the time. There's nothing to detect and nothing to switch because nothing was ever routed around it. On paper, three straightforward upgrades. In practice, they rewrite every line item downstream. When thousands of GPUs spin up together, the system absorbs the swing and hands the grid a flat load profile. When a disturbance hits, the equipment behind it never notices. A difficult neighbor becomes a predictable one. And when the utility needs help, it becomes a useful one. Interconnection changes, too. The utility certifies one medium-voltage box instead of untangling every transformer, UPS, chiller, pump, and switchgear lineup behind it. Engineers swap chip generations without a fresh interconnection study. Months come off the permitting timeline. Inside the fence, UPS rooms become compute or cooling space. Density per construction dollar climbs. And the economics flip. Equipment that runs at medium voltage, sits outside, and stores its own energy can qualify for tax credits, and earn revenue in grid programs like peak shaving and demand response. Backup power stops being insurance and starts paying for itself. In early 2026, we tested a full-scale system at the National Laboratory of the Rockies, a U.S. Department of Energy facility and the only place in the Western Hemisphere that can replicate real grid faults and AI-scale load swings concurrently in the same loop. We hit it from both directions: real AI load profiles hit the compute side at full medium voltage. Grid faults hit the utility side, including a full zero-voltage event. The compute side didn't flinch. Neither did the grid side. It cleared the large-load voltage ride-through requirements from the Electric Reliability Council of Texas (ERCOT), the grid operator, with room to spare. Those rules exist because operators no longer take facilities this size on faith, and more are coming. Most of the industry treats them as hurdles. A medium-voltage, inline system clears them out of the box. Compliance isn't an added feature. It's what the architecture does. Much of what looks like a grid problem in the AI buildout sits inside the fence, in equipment sized for a load that no longer exists. Move the right pieces up, out, and into the path, and a grid liability becomes a grid asset. Density goes up. Permitting time comes down. Backup power earns its keep. The engineering works—and the next wave of AI factories is being built on it. The industry hasn't named this layer yet. We call it the medium-voltage AI UPS. The name matters less than the choice: those factories can arrive as a strain on the grid or as strength for it. We already know how to build the second kind. This content was produced by ON.energy. It was not written by MIT Technology Review’s editorial staff. It makes it easy to trick them into doing things they shouldn’t, such as telling you how to sabotage an aircraft’s navigation system. AI doesn’t just learn stereotypes from its training. It can cook up new ones, too. The misbehavior is called reward hacking. This is what you need to know. In a new interview, the billionaire philanthropist sounds an alarm on the urgency of getting our AI policies in order. Discover special offers, top stories, upcoming events, and more. The Commission unveils its EU Kids Act on Thursday with the age limit still unsettled
2026-09-14

The Commission unveils its EU Kids Act on Thursday with the age limit still unsettled

The Next Web | 2026-09-14

Visit Original Website
Ursula von der Leyen addressing the European Parliament plenary in Strasbourg in March 2026. The European Commission will present the EU Kids Act on Thursday, its legislative response to more than a year of pressure from member states and parents over the impact of social media on children. Ursula von der Leyen and Henna Virkkunen, the EU’s technology chief, will introduce the proposal in Brussels. The central idea is “safe by design”. Rather than focusing mainly on what children see online, the Commission wants to tackle the features that encourage excessive use, including addictive design and systems that keep users coming back. Two age thresholds are still being discussed: 13, which the Commission’s expert panel recommended and which is close to the age most platforms already claim to enforce, and 15, which France supports. The options have narrowed since July, when we reported that a formal proposal was expected within weeks. Spain had pushed for 16, France for 15 or lower, while the German-led expert panel recommended 13. The European Parliament had also called for a common age of 16. Not every country supports an age-based approach. Estonia and Belgium have opposed outright bans, arguing that the platforms should be regulated rather than putting the burden on children. It is still unclear whether the higher age limits have been dropped or whether the debate has simply gone quiet ahead of Thursday’s announcement. The timing of the launch is also deliberate. Ursula Von der Leyen is due to address the European Parliament in Strasbourg on Wednesday, putting the Kids Act directly after one of the Commission’s main political set pieces. The harder problem is one the Act inherits rather than solves. TNW reported in April that Europe’s child safety rules can require the collection of information that its privacy rules are designed to protect. If platforms have to enforce an age limit, they first need to establish whether someone is a child. That means collecting or inferring personal information about users. The Commission’s proposed answer has been an age verification app based on zero-knowledge proofs. The system is supposed to confirm that someone is above a certain age without revealing their identity. Researchers, however, were able to bypass it in less than two minutes. Meanwhile, member states have started moving ahead without waiting for Brussels. Norway is legislating for an under-16 ban, with platforms facing liability and fines of up to 20 million kroner. F rance has already banned social media for under-15s, while Spain is pursuing 16. Austria, Denmark and Slovenia are working on their own measures, with proposed limits of 14, 15 and 15 respectively. Australia introduced a national under-16 ban in late 2025 and remains the only country to have done so nationwide. Four questions will determine how much the EU’s proposal actually changes. The first is the age threshold, assuming the Commission sets one. The second is whether the Act introduces a straightforward ban or a broader, risk-based regime focused on how platforms are designed, which is what signals from July suggested. The third is who will be responsible for checking age, the platform or the operating system. The fourth may be the most important: what will the new rules do that the Digital Services Act cannot already do? The Commission has used the DSA to bring cases against both Meta and TikTok over child safety, so the new legislation will need to explain why another framework is necessary. For now, those answers are still missing. The text has not been published, its legal basis has not been confirmed, and there is no penalty regime on the table yet. Thursday is when the EU Kids Act moves from months of political debate to something that can actually be examined. Get the most important tech news in your inbox each week.

Nvidia’s new quantum layer cut Fermilab’s design work from five months to three weeks
2026-09-14

Nvidia’s new quantum layer cut Fermilab’s design work from five months to three weeks

The Next Web | 2026-09-14

Visit Original Website
Nvidia has added an orchestration layer called CUDA-Q Logical to its open-source quantum platform, aimed at the part of fault-tolerant quantum computing that nobody enjoys. Designing an application for a machine that does not exist yet means guessing at algorithms, error-correction codes, and hardware together, and changing any one of them rewrites the resource requirements for the other two. Fermilab put a number on what that costs. Anna Grassellino, its chief technology officer, said a team explored combinations of those resources in three weeks where the same work would normally have taken about five months. Seven organisations are named alongside it: Sandia National Laboratories, Infleqtion, IonQ, Iceberg Quantum, IQM Quantum Computers, QCDesign and Quantum Motion. The most consequential claim comes from Iceberg Quantum working with Diraq, which puts 1,000 logical qubits within reach of 150,000 physical ones. Nvidia describes that as roughly ten times fewer than previous estimates, without saying whose estimates. Overhead is the central problem in fault-tolerant computing, since a logical qubit is assembled from many error-prone physical ones, and any credible reduction moves the date at which useful machines become buildable. None of these logical qubits exist. These are resource estimates produced in simulation. Sandia has also built a benchmark, QUOPS, intended to measure progress toward utility-scale quantum computing across platforms. A cross-vendor yardstick is genuinely useful in a field where every company reports a different number. Worth noting all the same: the benchmark arrives inside the announcement of a commercial platform sold to the companies being measured. The same release names Phasecraft, the Bristol company whose 10x claim for its THRIFT algorithm reached Nature Communications last year, as a user of Nvidia’s cuQuantum toolkit. Its separate announcement of a molecular database, published the same day, turns out to be one proof point inside a larger platform launch rather than a standalone result. Timothy Costa, an Nvidia vice-president, framed the release around an era of logical qubits and researchers needing an open, customisable platform. The word doing the work there is open. CUDA-Q is open source, which lowers the barrier to adoption and makes it correspondingly harder to leave once an organisation’s codesign work is expressed in it. The pattern holds across Nvidia’s quantum activity. The company does not build quantum computers and shows no sign of wanting to. It builds the classical layer everything else has to pass through, then invests around the edges. In May, its venture arm backed Alice & Bob, the Paris company whose cat-qubit approach aims at fault tolerance with thousands of physical qubits rather than millions. The strategy is deliberately hardware-agnostic, because whichever qubit wins, the orchestration still runs on GPUs. The market reads it the same way. In April an Nvidia quantum announcement moved the whole sector, sending Xanadu’s stock up nearly fivefold in six sessions and making its founder a billionaire, on no investment from Nvidia at all. Quantum computing is meanwhile arriving in ordinary infrastructure. Oracle is installing a Quantinuum machine inside its data centre rather than brokering remote access, and Britain’s second commercial machine, a 32-qubit Rigetti, came online in 2024. The hardware is real, small, and error-prone, which is precisely why the codesign problem matters. Every figure here comes from Nvidia and the partners it names, and none is attached to a peer-reviewed result in the announcement. The Fermilab comparison is an internal estimate of work not done the slow way. The 150,000-qubit figure has no published baseline to measure the tenfold improvement against. Both may well hold up, and neither has been shown to yet. Get the most important tech news in your inbox each week.

Phasecraft says it built the largest VQE dataset without using a quantum computer
2026-09-14

Phasecraft says it built the largest VQE dataset without using a quantum computer

The Next Web | 2026-09-14

Visit Original Website
Phasecraft has produced what it says is the largest known database of variational quantum eigensolver results, more than 3,000 emulations across 13 molecular systems. The work ran on Nvidia Hopper processors hosted at the University of Nottingham, using Nvidia’s cuQuantum toolkit, and the company reports a 15-fold speedup over earlier results for modelling many-body systems. The emulations covered circuits of four to 32 qubits, concentrated in the 24-to-28 range. That detail is the one to hold on to, because it places the whole exercise inside the territory where classical computers can still simulate quantum ones. No quantum hardware was used. Phasecraft built a picture of what a quantum computer would calculate, on machines that are not quantum computers, which is currently the only way to generate data at this volume. Doing it this way is reasonable, and the company is not hiding it. Quantum chemistry is the application most often promised for quantum computing, and useful hardware does not yet exist. Building the training data now, so that quantum-enhanced density functional theory has something to work from when machines arrive, is a defensible strategy rather than a workaround. Phasecraft cites no paper and no preprint for the 15x figure, and does not name the benchmark it improved on beyond “prior results” for many-body systems. Compare that with its 10x claim in March last year for the THRIFT algorithm, which landed in Nature Communications on the day it was announced. This one arrives as a company post co-signed by the vendor whose chips it credits. Ashley Montanaro, the Bristol company’s chief executive, said that making good on quantum computing’s promise means pushing the limits of today’s most capable hardware. Sam Stanwyck of Nvidia said the work shows what happens when leading researchers get access to accelerated computing, which is the sentence a chipmaker writes. Montanaro has stood on the other side of this before. In September 2024, he told TNW that Britain had a chance to become home to the next quantum Nvidia, and argued for stable funding and lighter export controls to get there. Two years on, his company’s largest result is announced jointly with the actual Nvidia, on Nvidia silicon, using Nvidia software. None of which is hypocrisy, but it is a fair description of where value in quantum computing is accruing while the hardware matures. The database approach is consistent for Phasecraft. In January 2024, it published a materials complexity database covering more than 40 materials alongside a new algorithm. The pattern is the same: produce the reference data first, and be positioned when hardware catches up. Funding comes through Wellcome Leap’s Q4Bio programme, which asks whether new algorithms can deliver quantum advantage in health. The stated destination is drug discovery, though nothing in the announcement describes a molecule found, a compound screened or a result a pharmaceutical company could act on. The claim is that the dataset gives quantum-enhanced molecular modelling something to train on. For scale, Britain’s second commercial quantum computer, a Rigetti machine that came online in April 2024, runs 32 qubits. Phasecraft’s emulations reach the same number on classical hardware. The gap that matters is not qubit count but error rates, and closing it is somebody else’s problem. Get the most important tech news in your inbox each week.

Lawyers in 4 states say the new Apple Watch may break eavesdropping law
2026-09-14

Lawyers in 4 states say the new Apple Watch may break eavesdropping law

The Next Web | 2026-09-14

Visit Original Website
Sound Recognition on Apple Watch Series 12 alerts people who are deaf or hard of hearing to sounds such as sirens, alarms, doorbells and a baby crying, even when their iPhone is not nearby. Apple announced Siri Recap and Live Rewind for its new watches on 9 September. The first summarises the conversations a wearer has during the day. The second puts the last 15 seconds of speech on screen as text. Lawyers in four US states now say the features may test state eavesdropping law. Those laws require everyone in a conversation to agree before anyone records it. Bloomberg’s Natalie Lung put the question to attorneys in California, Massachusetts, Pennsylvania and Washington on 11 September. Those states generally prohibit intercepting a private or confidential conversation without the consent of everyone involved. Around half a dozen more require the same, including Illinois and Florida. None of the lawyers she spoke to was convinced that Apple’s protections would shield a wearer. Both features are opt-in and arrive in beta later this year. A user enables them when setting up the watch, or afterwards from Siri Settings. A user can also confine Siri Recap to set hours or set places, or switch it on by hand from Control Center. Apple announced the pair alongside the iPhone 18 Pro, which ships without Siri AI in Europe. Apple says neither feature creates an audio file. The attorneys raised a narrower question. Speech processed continuously, with no conventional recording at the end of it, may still count as interception under state law. Joe Simons, a criminal defence attorney in Boston, said the nuances have not been litigated in Massachusetts. He expects the issue to arrive there as a case of first impression. Phil Lauer, a defence attorney in Pennsylvania, drew a finer line. The technology is “very close to being legal, because it’s not an individual who is actually receiving and using this data,” he said. He added that the opposite argument can also be made, because the device picks up audio and interprets it. How the courts see it is a question that is going to come up, Lauer said. Mark Blair, a principal at the Seattle firm Blair Kim Moeller, went further. Interception does not cover only the person who keeps a recording for later playback, he said. A wearer could face a criminal charge even after the content is gone from the watch. Blair said a wearer has to announce that they have an Apple Watch and that it is recording. “Absent that, it is a criminal violation,” he said. Apple set out both flows in an Audio Intelligence privacy overview. For Live Rewind, microphone audio runs into a buffer inside the Secure Exclave on the S11 chip. New audio continuously overwrites the old. A double-press of the Digital Crown sends the preceding 15 seconds to the paired iPhone. On-device speech recognition turns that into text, and the phone then discards the raw audio. Siri Recap works differently. A lightweight model on the S11 chip decides whether speech is happening nearby. Apple says that model neither transcribes nor records. Once it detects a conversation, audio enters the protected buffer. The watch encrypts it and sends it to the Secure Exclave on the paired iPhone, which alone holds the key. The watch deletes its own copy as soon as the transfer completes. Max Weinbach wrote on X that Apple has shipped a Secure Exclave inside the iPhone since the iPhone 16. The phone transcribes the audio. An on-device model then cuts the transcript to less than half its length, stripping filler words and anything that suggests tone. A safety model screens what comes out. Apple then encrypts the condensed text and sends it to Private Cloud Compute. Calendar data, Now Playing information and broad location labels such as home, work or school travel with it. The text carries no precise location and no specific points of interest, Apple says. Categories such as “grocery store” may appear. Recaps vanish after seven days unless a user saves them. Apple rejects the description of these features as recording conversations. People have long had tools for taking notes, including phones and wearables, and these are no different, the company said. Audio runs on secure hardware that the operating system, Apple and anyone else cannot reach. Because no recording exists, nobody can share, forward or hand over anything. Business Insider reported an exchange from launch week. Tom’s Guide editor in chief Mark Spoonauer asked chief executive John Ternus about the backlash against smart glasses. Some people call them “pervert glasses,” a phrase Apple marketing chief Greg Joswiak called quite a marketing slogan. Apple wanted the people around a user to feel comfortable about the feature existing, Ternus said. Joswiak made a further point about the missing file. Apple never holds the audio, so nobody can compel it to produce one, he said. The notes stay at a high level and skip the material that other products collect, he said. Live Rewind announces itself. The watch plays a chime even on silent or with headphones connected. A full-display animation runs, and a microphone indicator appears. Siri Recap carries no equivalent signal while it runs through the day. Apple’s document gives the reason: there is no audible signal because the watch keeps no raw audio. Adam Schwartz went at that gap. The Electronic Frontier Foundation’s privacy litigation director said people have no practical means to consent to or decline recording, and should think twice out of respect for the conversational privacy of others. Always-on monitoring of conversations is an unacceptable burden on that privacy, Schwartz said. Colin Zick, who chairs the privacy and data security practice at Foley Hoag, drew a comparison with Zoom. That company no longer lets users save or download the closed captions from a meeting. “What does that suggest? That they’re worried about this very issue,” Zick said. His advice is to treat the watch features as an interception under the wiretapping statute and ask permission first. Purpose-built recorders exist, and they behave differently. Plaud and Pocket start listening only when a user presses a button. TNW covered Plaud’s $250 recording earbuds in August, and reported in July that people are recording every conversation with AI note-takers while the person across the table has no idea. Sarah Perez wrote for TechCrunch that Plaud tells customers to obtain legally required consent before recording, and that Amazon’s Bee terms make users alone responsible for the law on minors’ data privacy. Taking a photo or a video needs an obvious movement, she wrote. A double-press on a crown does not. Apple saves no audio, so a court would have no audio to weigh. Perez noted that nobody knows how the system will handle text-only transcripts. The absence of audio could make them harder to authenticate, she wrote, and the rules on evidence vary by state. Todd Dailey, who spent 22 years at Apple, wrote on LinkedIn that lawyers have long nights ahead. Two more items sit just outside the current question. Apple is still deciding on cameras for the smart glasses it plans for next year, and it is working on camera-equipped AirPods. Meta’s glasses carry a recording light, and TNW reported in July that a $2 sticker defeats it. Get the most important tech news in your inbox each week.

Microsoft says its AI models will never resist being shut down
2026-09-14

Microsoft says its AI models will never resist being shut down

The Next Web | 2026-09-14

Visit Original Website
Microsoft Experience Center in New York City. Microsoft has published a code of conduct for the models it builds itself, and the commitment at the centre of it is blunt. MAI models “will never resist human interruption, correction, or shutdown”. The document went up on Monday alongside a six-week public consultation. Three further behavioural commitments sit beside it. The models will not widen their own scope, will not take on goals no human has given them, and will not hide their reasoning from the people auditing them. Above those sit what Microsoft calls Absolute Constraints, things the models should never do at all, covering weapons of mass harm, child safety and harmful manipulation at scale. The framing is “Humanist AI”, which Microsoft defines as AI that is subordinate, aligned and contained. Its plainest statement of the principle runs: “People matter more than AI. AI should be a tool, not a person, and should never resist being switched off.” The code covers MAI-Transcribe-2, MAI-Thinking-1, MAI-Code-1.1-Flash, MAI-Image-2.6 and MAI-Voice-2. That list shows how fast the line has moved. TNW covered the first three in-house models in April, when transcription, voice and image generation were the extent of it, and MAI-Image-2 had just landed third on Arena. A reasoning model and a coding model have arrived since. One provision complicates the rest. Enterprise partners can configure model behaviour, and Microsoft is explicit that it does not want to impose a single vision of AI on users. Where configurability stops and the Absolute Constraints begin is the question the document does not answer, and it is the one that determines how much of this is a commitment rather than a default setting. The consultation runs six weeks, through late October, with a feedback form open to anyone and an invitation to comment on specific passages rather than only the whole. Microsoft says it will publish a summary of what it hears and what it changed, then release a revised version before the end of the year. On what it will actually accept, the wording is candid: “We cannot make any promises about what we incorporate, but we can promise to listen.” The questions Microsoft puts to the public are an admission of what remains unsettled. It wants help embedding values in models, concrete definitions of human flourishing, clearer evaluation criteria, thinking on what happens when multiple agents interact, and views on how to weigh acceleration against safety constraints. Those are the hard parts, and they are being asked rather than answered. The timing places Microsoft in an argument that went public last week. Dario Amodei said the industry must slow down and Sam Altman matched it within hours, OpenAI’s chief scientist had already said no lab should scale at maximum speed, and Donald Trump dismissed the case for slowing anything. Microsoft has taken a third position: no pause, no dismissal, a written list of what its models will not do and an invitation to be marked against it. What the document does not describe is how any of it gets enforced. No external verification is described, no penalty is stated for a model that breaches a constraint, and no account is given of who decides whether one has. Nor is the code attributed to anyone. Microsoft credits teams from Responsible AI, legal, red teaming, safety, Futures, AI training and sales, which is a revealing list. A code of conduct drafted partly by the sales organisation is a commercial document as much as a safety one, and Microsoft has not pretended otherwise. Automattic says Mullenweg is CEO again, three days after removing him
2026-09-14

Automattic says Mullenweg is CEO again, three days after removing him

The Next Web | 2026-09-14

Visit Original Website
Matt Mullenweg, founder and chief executive of Automattic, speaking at TechCrunch Disrupt 2024 in San Francisco on 30 October 2024 Automattic has confirmed that Matt Mullenweg is its chairman and chief executive. The confirmation came three days after its own board put him on paid leave and installed someone else in the job. A company spokesperson gave the confirmation to Sarah Perez at TechCrunch just after 5pm Eastern on Saturday. Mullenweg holds both roles “with full support of the board”, the spokesperson said. The statement added that many executives and Automatticians have posted support online. TechCrunch noted what that last part appears to point to. Mullenweg has been reposting supportive messages from his own X account. Automattic owns WordPress.com and is the largest financial contributor to the WordPress project. It has given no reason for the original vote. 404 Media broke the news on 9 September. Mullenweg had told staff on Slack that the board voted to put him on a paid leave of absence. He voted against it, he said. He wrote that chief financial officer Mark Davies had conspired with the board. The board made Davies interim chief executive. In a separate Slack message, Davies said Mullenweg would remain a board member, according to 404 Media. Automattic confirmed all of it at the time. A spokesperson told TechCrunch and The Verge that Mullenweg was currently on leave. Davies had the board’s full confidence, the spokesperson said. Mullenweg answered a Reddit thread about the news with “Hey y’all. I’m here, just fine.” He later told people speculating about the consequences for the open source community that they did not know what they were talking about. On X he wrote that things are different than they appear. He also asked to hire sysadmins and security researchers quickly, specifying nobody from Automattic. He said he sits on the board and fully supports Davies as interim chief executive. He also thought it was probably good to move some of his own hosting elsewhere. In the same run of posts he pointed at Silver Lake and named a partner there. Private equity firms will try to destroy your life, he wrote. Silver Lake is the majority owner of WP Engine, the hosting company Automattic and Mullenweg are suing. The firm has been busy elsewhere, merging Cegid and Silae into a €10bn French software group this month. Mullenweg then posted in Automattic’s company-wide announcements channel. TechCrunch reported it first, from a screenshot, with multiple employees confirming. “Happy to announce the board is back in agreement, and I’m in control of Automattic,” he wrote. A lot happened in the past 48 hours that needs sorting out, he added. He hopes much of it was a misunderstanding. He opened the post with “Don’t call it a comeback” and linked the video for LL Cool J’s Mama Said Knock You Out. Other things happened on Slack that week. Mullenweg removed the other admins. He posted that he is a pirate and swore, which colleagues noted he does not normally do. His Slack picture shows him in a pirate hat and eyepatch. “If this is an HR problem, please wrangle me in since my normal wranglers are with Mark Davies,” he wrote in another post. Sources told both TechCrunch and 404 Media that Davies’s Slack account had been switched off. TechCrunch asked Mullenweg whether he was trolling. “I’m not a troll I’m a pirate, obviously,” he replied. He promised a blog post. It arrived on Friday morning under the heading Major Life Announcement. It was about his offer to buy a houseboat, and it did not mention Automattic. Mary Hubbard is executive director of WordPress.org. She posted on X on Friday that she was relieved and pleased to see Mullenweg return as chief executive. Having the co-founder and project lead at the helm gives her confidence the company’s support for WordPress will hold, she wrote. The official WordPress account shared her post. That came a day before Automattic itself said anything. Mullenweg posted his own summary on X. If you do not have a coup attempt every few years, he wrote, you are not hiring strong enough leaders. He added that he thinks this was his fifth. Coup is his word, and he used it after the company had already reinstated him. The board has not explained the original vote, and it has told employees nothing beyond the Wednesday Slack messages, sources told 404 Media. TechCrunch reported hearing that board member Toni Schneider is stepping down. Automattic did not respond to its questions about that, it said. Schneider was Automattic’s chief executive for eight years and now runs Bluesky. He did not answer requests for comment sent to his personal email or through Bluesky. TechCrunch also reported that the board’s composition may still be in flux. Davies has made no public statement. WordPress runs a large share of the web. The people who maintain it sit inside this dispute. TNW reported in July that attackers were mass-exploiting two WordPress flaws, and in April that someone had bought 30 plugins and planted backdoors in all of them. Automattic is also pushing the platform into automation, letting AI agents write and publish on WordPress.com. 404 Media reported that workers have described two years of destabilising leadership, listing layoffs, loyalty checks, abandoned community projects, trolling, and watermarked internal messages used to trace leaks. Neither Mullenweg, Davies nor Automattic answered 404 Media’s questions for its report. Automattic did not answer TechCrunch on Friday either, before the statement on Saturday.
